Higher homological algebra for one-point extensions of bipartite hereditary algebras and spectral graph theory
Karin M. Jacobsen, Mads Hustad Sandøy, Laertis Vaso

Abstract
. In this article we study higher homological properties of n n -levelled algebras and connect them to properties of the underlying graphs. Notably, to each 2 2 -representation-finite quadratic monomial algebra Λ \Lambda we associate a bipartite graph B Λ ¯ \overline{B_{\Lambda}} and we classify all such algebras Λ \Lambda for which B Λ ¯ \overline{B_{\Lambda}} is regular or edge-transitive. We also show that if B Λ ¯ \overline{B_{\Lambda}} is semi-regular, then it is a reflexive graph.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
16G20, 16E65, 16S37, 05C25

1.
Introduction

Background

A celebrated result of Gabriel classifies hereditary representation-finite algebras over an algebraically closed field
\K
by the Dynkin diagrams
[
Gab72
]
. The representation theory of these algebras is completely understood and can be described using Auslander–Reiten theory, see for example
[
ARS97
,
ASS06
]
.

In 2004 Iyama introduced an
n
-dimensional version of Auslander–Reiten theory where
n
is a positive integer
[
Iya07b
,
Iya08
]
. By applying this theory one obtains the class of
n
-representation-finite algebras that can be seen as certain generalisations of hereditary representation-finite algebras. Examples of
n
-representation-finite algebras include higher Auslander algebras of type
A

[
Iya11
]
and algebras of the form
\Lambda_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda_{k}
where each
\Lambda_{i}
is an
\ell
-homogeneous
n_{i}
-representation-finite algebra over a perfect field
\K

[
HI11a
, Corollary 1.5]
.

Although higher dimensional Auslander–Reiten theory has found applications in fields as diverse as algebraic geometry
[
IW11
,
IW13
,
IW14
,
HIMO23
,
JKM23
]
, combinatorics
[
OT12
,
HJ21
,
Wil22
]
, higher category theory and algebraic K-theory
[
DJW19
]
and symplectic geometry
[
DJL21
,
Di 23
]
, and although many classical results from representation theory have been generalised in this setting
[
Iya07a
,
HIO14
,
Jør14
]
, it seems that an explicit classification of
n
-representation-finite algebras for
n>1
is currently out of reach. However, some more specialised versions of this question have been adressed by imposing additional constrains, e.g.
[
DI20
,
Vas23
,
HJS22
]
. In particular, in
[
HI11b
]
Herschend and Iyama show that all basic
2
-representation-finite algebras are described using cuts of selfinjective quivers with potential and examples of such nature are given in
[
Pas20
,
Pet19
]
.

In a similar manner the authors of
[
HST24
]
tried to classify
n
-representation-finite quadratic monomial bound quiver algebras. They gave a complete classification when
n>2
and in the case
n=2
they showed that if a quadratic monomial bound quiver algebra
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
is
2
-representation-finite, then the quiver
Q
is a
star quiver
S_{(r,s)}
given by

(1.1)

x_{1}
x_{r}
y_{1}
y_{s}
.
z
a_{1}
a_{r}
b_{1}
b_{s}

Main results

One aim of this article is to continue the classification in
[
HST24
]
by studying which of these algebras are indeed
2
-representation-finite. Note that the data defining a quadratic monomial bound quiver algebra
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
is equivalent to that of a quiver
B_{\Lambda}
with vertices
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
and arrows
\beta_{ji}:y_{j}\to x_{i}
whenever
a_{i}b_{j}\not\in\I
. Note that the underlying graph
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is bipartite with partition given by
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
and
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
; if all of the vertices
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
have the same degree
\Sigma_{1}
and all of the vertices
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
have the same degree
\Sigma_{2}
, then
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
his called
semi-regular of bidegree
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
. Our main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1

(Corollary
5.10
)
.

Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}
be a
2
-representation-finite
(r,s)
-star algebra. If the underlying graph
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
of
B_{\Lambda}
is semi-regular of bidegree
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
, then

(a)

r\Sigma_{1}=s\Sigma_{2}
, and

(b)

\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+r+s-r\Sigma_{1}\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.

By solving the system of linear Diophantine equations given by parts (a) and (b) of Theorem
1.1
and using the existing literature on graph theory, we can give the following classification result.

Theorem 1.2

(Proposition
5.12
and Proposition
5.14
)
.

Assume that
\charac(\K)=0
. Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be a
2
-representation-finite
(r,s)
-star algebra.

(i)

If
B_{\Lambda}
is regular, then the underlying graph
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is one of the following:

(a)

the bipartite complement of the path graph
P_{2}
;

(b)

the cycle graph
C_{8}
;

(c)

the Heawood graph (G-5 in the Encyclopedia of graphs
[
EoG24
]
) or its bipartite complement.

(ii)

If
B_{\Lambda}
is edge-transitive, then the underlying graph
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is one of graphs appearing in (a)—(c) or one of the following:

(d)

the graph G-9P730 in the Encyclopedia of Graphs or its bipartite complement;

(e)

the graph G-9P731 in the Encyclopedia of Graphs or its bipartite complement.

We refer the interested reader to (
5.3
)–(
5.10
) for a detailed description of these
2
-representation-finite algebras. We note that in all examples we know of a quadratic monomial algebra
\Lambda
being
2
-representation-finite, the graph
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is both edge-transitive and semi-regular. We thus pose the following question.

Question 1
.

Does there exist a
2
-representation-finite quadratic monomial algebra
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
such that
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is not semi-regular or edge-transitive?

If the answer to Question
1
is negative, then Theorem
1.2
provides a classification of all
2
-representation-finite quadratic monomial algebras when
\charac(\K)=0
.

Applications

Theorem
1.1
provides an unexpected connection between representation theory, graph theory and linear Diophantine equations. Before we mention one application of this connection, let us recall that a graph whose second largest eigenvalue is less than or equal to
2
is called a
reflexive
graph
[
KS14
]
; if furthermore the graph is bipartite and the largest eigenvalue is strictly greater than
2
then the graph is called a
Salem
graph
[
MS05
]
. Salem graphs can be thought of as a generalization of the Dynkin and extended Dynkin diagrams.

Theorem 1.3

(Corollary
5.13
)
.

Assume that
\charac(\K)=0
. Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be a
2
-representation-finite
(r,s)
-star algebra. Assume that
\Lambda
is semi-regular. Then
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is a reflexive graph. Moreover,
\overline{B_{\Lambda}}
is a Salem graph if and only if
(r,s)\neq(1,1)
and
(r,s)\neq(4,4)
.

Most of our work is done in a more general setting where
S_{(r,s)}
is replaced by an
n
-levelled quiver and
2
-representation-finiteness is replaced by being twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau; the results in this general setting may be of independent interest.

Outline of the paper

In Section
2
we recall the necessary background for this article. In Section
3
we study
n
-levelled algebras from the point of view of higher homological algebra. In Section
4
we study one-point extensions of bipartite hereditary algebras and explain how they are related to star quivers. In Section
5
we focus on
2
-representation-finite quadratic monomial algebras and prove the main results of this article. In Appendix
B
we investigate which solutions of the system of linear Diophantine equations found in Appendix
A
give rise to
2
-representation-finite algebras in the regular and edge-transitive case.

2.
Background

2.1.
Conventions and notation

For a positive integer
d\geq 1
, we denote by
I_{d}
or simply
I
the
d\times d
identity matrix and by
0_{d}
or simply
0
the
d\times d
zero matrix. We write the composition of morphisms

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z

as
g\circ f
.

We fix an algebraically closed field
\K
. Throughout this paper, by an algebra we mean a finite-dimensional associative
\K
-algebra and by a
\Lambda
-module we mean a finitely generated right
\Lambda
-module. All quivers in this article are connected unless stated otherwise. For standard results on representation theory of algebras and quivers we refer the reader to
[
ARS97
,
ASS06
]
.

For a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
, we let
\modu\Lambda
be the category of finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-modules. Similarly,
\gr\Lambda
denotes the category of finite-dimensional graded
\Lambda
-modules with morphisms consisting of homogeneous homomorphisms of degree zero. Given a graded
\Lambda
-module
M=\oplus_{i\in\ZZ}M_{i}
, we define the
j
-th graded shift
of
M
to be the graded
\Lambda
-module
M\langle j\rangle
with
M\langle j\rangle_{i}=M_{i-j}
. We denote by
D
the duality
D(-)=\Hom_{\K}(-,\K)
between
\modu\Lambda
and
\modu\Lambda^{\text{op}}
. For a
\Lambda
-module
M
we denote by
\pdim(M)
respectively
\idim(M)
the projective, respectively injective, dimension of
M
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. We write the composition of arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\xrightarrow{\alpha}j\xrightarrow{\beta}k}

in
Q
as
\alpha\beta
. The underlying graph of the quiver
Q
will be denoted by
\overline{Q}
.

Given a bound quiver algebra
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
and
M\in\modu\Lambda
, the
dimension vector of
M
is defined to be the vector
\dimv(M)
given by

\dimv(M)=(\dim_{\K}(Me_{1}),\dots,\dim_{\K}(Me_{n}))^{T},

where
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
are the idempotents of
\Lambda
corresponding to the vertices of
Q
. We denote the
Loewy length
of
M
by
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(M)
or simply
\ell\ell(M)
when the algebra
\Lambda
is clear from context. We use the convention that the
Cartan matrix

C_{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda
is given by the matrix consisting of the dimension vectors of the indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-modules arranged in order from top to bottom as row vectors.
Given this, the
Coxeter matrix
of
\Lambda
is given by
-C_{\Lambda}^{T}C_{\Lambda}^{-1}
. The
Coxeter polynomial

\CP_{\Lambda}(t)
of
\Lambda
is the characteristic polynomial of the Coxeter matrix of
\Lambda
, that is

\CP_{\Lambda}(t)=\det(tI-(-C_{\Lambda}^{T}C_{\Lambda}^{-1})).

Note that the Coxeter polynomial of an algebra
\Lambda
is a monic integer polynomial that is self-reciprocal, i.e. its coefficients form a palindromic sequence
[
Luk92
]
, or, equivalently, it is a polynomial
\CP_{\Lambda}(t)
of degree
n
satisfying

\CP_{\Lambda}(t)=t^{n}\CP_{\Lambda}(t^{-1}).

2.2.
Spectral graph theory

By a
graph
we mean a simple undirected graph
G=(V,E)
where
V
is the set of vertices and
E
is the set of edges. All graphs in this article are
finite
, that is
V
and
E
are finite sets. To any graph
G
one can associate its
adjacency matrix

A(G)
by choosing some ordering on the vertices
V
and setting
(A(G))_{i,j}=1
when there is an edge between
i
and
j
and letting
(A(G))_{i,j}=0
otherwise. Clearly, the adjacency matrix of a graph is symmetric, and since it is also real, its spectrum is real as well. Hence, its eigenvalues are ordered, say as
\lambda_{n}\leq\lambda_{n-1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{1}
.

Recall that a graph
G
is called
bipartite
if there is a partition
V=X\cup Y
such that every edge has one endpoint in
X
and the other in
Y
. We call such a partition a
coloring
of
G
. It is easy to see that a graph
G
is bipartite if and only if the adjacency matrix of
G
can be given in the form

A(G)=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}

with
R
some
r\times s
-matrix with zeroes and ones. The spectrum of a bipartite graph is symmetric about the origin, and this property in fact characterizes the class of bipartite graphs.

The
degree
of a vertex of a graph is the number of edges incident with it.
A graph
G
is
regular
if every vertex has the same degree.
A bipartite graph
G=(X\cup Y,E)
is
semi-regular
if every vertex in
X
has degree
m\in\mathbb{N}
and every vertex in
Y
has degree
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and in this case we say that
G
has
bidegree

(m,n)
. Note that in this case we have
m\lvert X\rvert=n\lvert Y\rvert
by counting the number of edges in the graph.

The graphs whose largest eigenvalue is less than or equal to
2
are called
Smith
or
cyclotomic
graphs and have been classified: they coincide with the class of Dynkin and extended Dynkin diagrams
[
Smi69
]
. The case where the bound is sharp corresponds to the Dynkin case, whereas equality corresponds to the extended Dynkin case. All but one of the classes of Smith graphs are bipartite. The graphs whose second largest eigenvalue is less than or equal to
2
form another class of much studied graphs called
reflexive
graphs
[
KS14
]
. Furthermore, bipartite reflexive graphs whose largest eigenvalue is strictly greater than
2
are called
Salem
graphs
[
MS05
]
. These notions have connections to hyperbolic geometry
[
NS83
]
.

Given a bipartite graph
G=(X\cup Y,E)
one can consider
its bipartite complement
, namely the graph obtained by taking the same vertex set as
G
but having edge set consisting of every edge going between
X
and
Y
that is
not
in
E
. For an example, see figure
1
.

x_{3}
y_{2}
x_{2}
y_{1}
x_{1}
y_{7}
x_{7}
y_{6}
x_{6}
y_{5}
x_{5}
y_{4}
x_{4}
y_{3}
x_{3}
y_{2}
x_{2}
y_{1}
x_{1}
y_{7}
x_{7}
y_{6}
x_{6}
y_{5}
x_{5}
y_{4}
x_{4}
y_{3}

Figure 1.
On the left, a bipartite graph known as the
Heawood graph
. On the right, its bipartite complement.

A
loopless multigraph

G
is a graph with possibly multiple parallel edges, but with no edges starting and ending in the same vertex. Equivalently, it is a graph
G=(V,E)
with a weight function
w\colon E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. The degree of a vertex
v
is still defined to be the number of edges adjacent to
v
(or, equivalently, the sum
\sum w(e)
where
e
ranges among all edges adjacent to
v
).
Consequently, it makes sense to speak of bipartite loopless multigraphs as well as of regular and semi-regular versions of those. Note however that there is no natural definition of a bipartite complement for loopless multigraphs.
The adjacency matrix
A(G)
of such a graph
G
has
(A(G))_{i,j}
equal to the number of edges between vertices
i
and
j
.
Of course, this is a symmetric integer matrix, one can consider its spectrum, its Perron-Frobenius eigenvector, and so on as usual.
Note also that the facts listed above for the spectrum of a bipartite graph also hold in this generality.

2.3.
Bi-eigenvectors and bi-eigenvalues

Let
G=(X\cup Y,E)
be a semi-regular graph of bidegree
(m,n)
. Assume that
\lvert X\rvert=r
and
\lvert Y\rvert=s
. Let
A=A(G)=\begin{bmatrix}0&R\\
R^{T}&0\end{bmatrix}
be the adjacency matrix of
A
. Then we have

\begin{bmatrix}0&R\\
R^{T}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{r+s}=\begin{bmatrix}m\\
\vdots\\
m\\
n\\
\vdots\\
n\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}m\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{r}\\
n\;\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

We want to study this eigenvector-like behaviour of the all-ones vector with respect to the adjacency matrix of a semi-regular graph. Motivated by this, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.1
.

Let

A=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}

be a square
\CC
-matrix, let
d_{r}
be an
r\times 1

\CC
-vector, let
d_{s}
be an
s\times 1

\CC
-vector and let
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\CC
.
We say that
d=\begin{bmatrix}d_{r}\\
d_{s}\end{bmatrix}
is a
bi-eigenvector of
A
with bi-eigenvalue
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
if
d
is nonzero and both
Rd_{s}=\Sigma_{1}d_{r}
and
R^{T}d_{r}=\Sigma_{2}d_{s}
hold, or, equivalently, if it is nonzero and

\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}d_{r}\\
d_{s}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}d_{r}\\
\Sigma_{2}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

Example 2.2
.

Let

A=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

Then if
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{r+s})^{T}
is an eigenvector of
A
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, we have that
((v_{1},\dots,v_{r})^{T},(v_{r+1},\dots,v_{r+s})^{T})
is a bi-eigenvector of
A
with bi-eigenvalue
(\lambda,\lambda)
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let

A=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}

be a square
\CC
-matrix. Let
(d_{r},d_{s})
be a bi-eigenvector of
A
with bi-eigenvalue
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
. Then the following hold.

(a)

We have
RR^{T}d_{r}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}d_{r}
. In particular, if
d_{r}\neq 0
, then
d_{r}
is an eigenvector of
RR^{T}
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
.

(b)

We have
R^{T}Rd_{s}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}d_{s}
. In particular, if
d_{s}\neq 0
, then
d_{s}
is an eigenvector of
R^{T}R
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
.

(c)

We have
\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{r}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{s}\rvert^{2}
.

Proof.

Parts (a) and (b) follow immediately from
Rd_{s}=\Sigma_{1}d_{r}
and
R^{T}d_{r}=\Sigma_{2}d_{s}
. For part (c) we have

\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{r}\rvert^{2}=d_{r}^{T}(\Sigma_{1}d_{r})=d_{r}^{T}(Rd_{s})=(R^{T}d_{r})^{T}d_{s}=\Sigma_{2}d_{s}^{T}d_{s}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{s}\rvert^{2}.\qed

We are especially interested in nonnegative integer bi-eigenvectors of adjacency matrices of bipartite loopless multigraphs.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
G
be a bipartite loopless multigraph with adjacency matrix

A=A(G)=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix},

and assume that
G
has at least one edge. Let
(d_{r},d_{s})
be a bi-eigenvector of
A
with nonnegative integer entries and with bi-eigenvalue
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
. Then the following hold.

(a)

\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\QQ
,
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\in\ZZ
and
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}>0
.

(b)

d_{r}\neq 0
and
d_{s}\neq 0
. In particular,
d_{r}
is an eigenvector of
RR^{T}
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
and
d_{s}
is an eigenvector of
R^{T}R
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
.

(c)

\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{r}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{s}\rvert^{2}
.

(d)

\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}
is an eigenvector of
A
with eigenvalue
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
.

(e)

The largest eigenvalue of
A
is
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
.

Proof.

(a)

Since
R
,
d_{r}
and
d_{s}
have integer entries, it follows that
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\QQ
. Next, by Lemma
2.3
(a) and (b) we have

RR^{T}d_{r}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}d_{r}
and
R^{T}Rd_{s}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}d_{s}
.

Since the entries of
R
are nonnegative integers, it follows that
RR^{T}
and
R^{T}R
both have nonnegative integer entries. Since
G
has at least one edge, we also have that
RR^{T}\neq 0
and
R^{T}R\neq 0
. Since both
d_{r}
and
d_{s}
have nonnegative integer entries, but by definition
d_{r}
and
d_{s}
cannot both be zero vectors, it follows that
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
is a positive integer.

(b)

By definition we have
Rd_{s}=\Sigma_{1}d_{r}
and
R^{T}d_{r}=\Sigma_{2}d_{s}
. Since by part (a) we have
\Sigma_{1}\neq 0
and
\Sigma_{2}\neq 0
, and since by definition at least one of
d_{r}
and
d_{s}
is nonzero, it follows that the other one also is nonzero. The claim about eigenvectors and eigenvalues follows by Lemma
2.3
(a) and (b).

(c)

Follows by Lemma
2.3
(c).

(d)

By part (a) we have that
\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}
is nonzero. Then we compute

\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}R\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\\
R^{T}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{r}\\
\Sigma_{2}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}=\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

(e)

By (d) we have that
\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}
has
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
as an eigenvalue. Since
\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}}d_{r}\\
\sqrt{\Sigma_{2}}d_{s}\end{bmatrix}
has nonnegative entries, it follows by the Perron–Frobenius theorem that
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
is largest in absolute value among the eigenvalues of
A
. Since all eigenvalues of
A
are real and
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
is positive, the claim follows. ∎

2.4.
Koszul algebras

Recall that a graded algebra
\Lambda=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\Lambda_{i}
with semisimple degree
0
part is a
Koszul algebra
if
\Lambda_{0}
satisfies

\Ext^{i}_{\gr\Lambda}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}\langle j\rangle)=0

whenever
i\neq j

[
BGS96
, Proposition 2.1.3]
. In that case, we call

\Lambda^{!}=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\Ext_{\gr\Lambda}^{i}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{0}\langle i\rangle)

the
Koszul dual of
\Lambda
. If
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
is a bound quiver algebra, then the quiver of
\Lambda^{!}
is given by the opposite quiver of
Q
; see e.g.
[
BGS96
, Definition 2.8.1 and Proposition 2.10.1]
.

Example 2.5
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a bound quiver algebra, where the ideal
\I
is generated by quadratic relations and
\gldim\Lambda\leq 2
.
Then
\Lambda
is Koszul; see e.g.
[
MV07
, Proposition 2.19]
.

2.5.
Higher-dimensional homological algebra

We recall some notions and results in higher-dimensional homological algebra from
[
Iya08
,
HI11a
,
HIO14
]
. Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. We denote by

\tau_{n}\coloneqq\tau\Omega^{n-1}:\modu\Lambda\to\modu\Lambda\text{ and }\tau_{n}^{-}\coloneqq\tau^{-}\Omega^{-(n-1)}:\modu\Lambda\to\modu\Lambda

the
n
-Auslander–Reiten translations
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
M\in\modu\Lambda
and
n\geq 1
be an integer. We say that
M
is an
n
-cluster tilting
module if

\displaystyle\add(M)

\displaystyle=\{N\in\modu\Lambda\mid\Ext^{i}_{\Lambda}(M,N)=0\text{ for all $0<i<n$}\}

\displaystyle=\{N\in\modu\Lambda\mid\Ext^{i}_{\Lambda}(N,M)=0\text{ for all $0<i<n$}\}.

Note that if
M\in\modu\Lambda
is an
n
-cluster tilting module and
X\in\add(M)
is non-projective, then
\pdim(X)\geq n
; similarly if
X\in\add(M)
is non-injective, then
\idim(X)\geq n
. Now assume that
\Lambda
has finite global dimension. We denote by

\nu=\nu_{\Lambda}\coloneqq(D\Lambda)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Lambda}-:\db(\modu\Lambda)\to\db(\modu\Lambda)

the
(derived) Nakayama functor
and by

\nu^{-}=\nu^{-}_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\mathbf{R}\Hom_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda,-):\db(\modu\Lambda)\to\db(\modu\Lambda)

its quasi-inverse; for more details see
[
Hap88
]
.
It is well-known that
\nu
is a Serre functor of
\db(\modu\Lambda)
whenever
\Lambda
has finite global dimension.
We denote
\nu_{n}=\nu\circ[-n]
.
Then we have the formulas

(2.1)

\tau_{n}=H^{0}(\nu_{n}(-)):\modu\Lambda\to\modu\Lambda\text{ and }\tau_{n}^{-}=H^{0}(\nu_{n}^{-}(-)):\modu\Lambda\to\modu\Lambda,

see
[
HIO14
]
. We also use the following result from
[
HIO14
]
.

Proposition 2.7
.

[
HIO14
, Proposition 2.3]
Let
M\in\modu\Lambda
.

(a)

If
\nu_{n}^{i}(M)\in\modu\Lambda
for some
i>0
, then
\nu_{n}^{j}(M)\in\modu\Lambda
for all
0\leq j\leq i
.

(b)

If
\nu_{n}^{-i}(M)\in\modu\Lambda
for some
i>0
, then
\nu_{n}^{-j}(M)\in\modu\Lambda
for all
0\leq j\leq i
.

With this setup in mind, we recall the following definitions.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
n\geq 1
be a positive integer and
\Lambda
a finite-dimensional algebra.

(a)

[
HIO14
, Proposition 2.6]
We say that
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-finite
if for every indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-module
P\in\modu\Lambda
there exists some
i\geq 0
such that
\nu_{n}^{-i}(P)
is an indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-module.

(b)

[
HIO14
, Definition 2.7]
We say that
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-infinite
if
\gldim\Lambda\leq n
and for every indecomposable projective module
P\in\modu\Lambda
and for any
i\geq 0
we have that
\nu_{n}^{-i}(P)\in\modu\Lambda
.

(c)

[
HIO14
, Theorem 3.4]
We say that
\Lambda
is
n
-hereditary
if
\Lambda
is either
n
-representation-finite or
n
-representation-infinite.

We collect some important results for
n
-hereditary algebras in the following remark.

Remark 2.9
.

(a)

By
[
HIO14
, Proposition 2.6]
we have that
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-finite if and only if
M=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\tau_{n}^{-i}(\Lambda)
is an
n
-cluster tilting module and
\gldim(\Lambda)\leq n
. Let
X\in\add(M)
be non-projective. Since
\pdim(X)\geq n
, we conclude that
\pdim(X)=n
. Similarly, if
X\in\add(M)
is non-injective, then
\idim(X)=n
.

(b)

Assume that
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-finite. Then the functor
\tau_{n}
gives a bijection from the isomorphism classes of indecomposable non-projective modules in
\add(M)
to the isomorphism classes of indecomposable non-injective modules in
\add(M)
, with inverse given by
\tau_{n}^{-}
, see
[
Iya08
, Theorem 2.8]
. In this case we obtain

M=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\tau_{n}^{-i}(\Lambda)=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\tau_{n}^{i}(D\Lambda).

In particular, for each indecomposable projective module
P\in\modu\Lambda
, there exists a unique
\ell_{P}>0
such that
\tau_{n}^{-(\ell_{P}-1)}(P)
is indecomposable injective and
\tau_{n}^{-\ell_{P}}(P)=0
. If for all indecomposable projective modules
P,P^{\prime}\in\modu\Lambda
we have
\ell_{P}=\ell_{P^{\prime}}=:\ell
, then we say that
\Lambda
is
\ell
-homogeneous
see
[
HI11a
, Definition 1.2]
. Thus, in general, if
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
is a bound quiver algebra, then we obtain

\bullet

a bijection
\sigma:Q_{0}\to Q_{0}
, and

\bullet

for each vertex
v\in Q_{0}
a positive integer
\ell_{v}

such that
\tau_{n}^{-(\ell_{v}-1)}(e_{v}\Lambda)\simeq D(\Lambda e_{\sigma(v)})
and
\tau_{n}^{\ell_{v}-1}(D\Lambda e_{v})\simeq e_{\sigma^{-1}(v)}\Lambda
.

(c)

By part (a) and the definition of
n
-hereditary algebras, it follows that if
\Lambda
is
n
-hereditary, then
\gldim(\Lambda)\leq n
.

(d)

Let
M\in\modu\Lambda
be a module and assume that
\nu_{n}^{-i}(M)\in\modu\Lambda
. Then by (
2.1
) we have that

(2.2)

\tau_{n}^{-i}(M)=H^{0}(\nu_{n}^{-i}(M))=\nu_{n}^{-i}(M).

Assume that
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-finite. Let
P\in\modu\Lambda
be indecomposable projective. Then by (
2.2
) and Proposition
2.7
we obtain that

(2.3)

\tau_{n}^{-j}(P)=\nu_{n}^{-j}(P)

for
0\leq j\leq\ell_{P}-1
.
Similarly, if
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-infinite we have that (
2.3
) holds for all
j\geq 0
. Corresponding statements hold by replacing projective with injective,
\tau_{n}^{-}
with
\tau_{n}
and
\nu_{n}^{-}
with
\nu_{n}
.

We now recall two more definitions.

Definition 2.10
.

[
HI11a
]

Let
\Lambda
be an
n
-representation-finite algebra. We denote by

\Pi=\Pi(\Lambda)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in\ZZ}\Hom_{\derived}(\Lambda,\nu_{n}^{-i}(\Lambda))

the
(n+1)
-preprojective algebra
.

Definition 2.11
.

[
HI11a
, Def 0.3]

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. We say that
\Lambda
is
twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau of dimension
\tfrac{m}{l}
if the functor
\nu^{l}
is isomorphic to the functor
(-)_{\phi}\circ[m]
for some integers
m
and
l\neq 0
and
\phi
an automorphism of
\Lambda
.

The following general statements concerning twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau algebras are important for us.

Theorem 2.12
.

[
HI11a
, Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.3]

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional connected algebra, and let
\ell
and
n
be positive integers.

(a)

If
\Lambda
is
n
-representation-finite, then
\Lambda
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau.

(b)

\Lambda
is
\ell
-homogeneous
n
-representation-finite if and only if
\Lambda
is twisted
\tfrac{n(\ell-1)}{\ell}
-Calabi–Yau and
\gldim(\Lambda)\leq n
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\Lambda
be a twisted fractionally-Calabi–Yau algebra of finite global dimension. Then all the eigenvalues of the Coxeter matrix of
\Lambda
are on the unit circle.

Proof.

We follow
[
Han24
, Remark 1]
. Let the Calabi–Yau dimension of
\Lambda
be
\tfrac{m}{l}
.
By
[
HI11a
, Proposition 4.3(a)]
we have that
\nu^{l}(\Lambda)\simeq\Lambda[m]
.
Hence
\nu^{l}\circ[-m]
induces a group automorphism of the Grothendieck group of the derived category of
\Lambda
with basis given by the indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-modules.
Moreover, the corresponding matrix
[\nu^{l}\circ[-m]]
is a permutation matrix up to a sign and so has eigenvalues on the unit circle.
The eigenvalues of
[\nu^{l}\circ[-m]]
are, up to sign, equal to the eigenvalues of
[\nu^{l}]
, which are
l
-th powers of the eigenvalues of
[\nu]
, see
[
Ber09
, Proposition 4.4.5]
. It follows that the eigenvalues of
[\nu\circ[-1]]
are on the unit circle. Since the Coxeter matrix corresponds to the induced action of the functor
\nu\circ[-1]
on the Grothendieck group of the derived category of
\Lambda
(see e.g. point (iii) at the start of the first section in
[
LdlPn08
]
), the claim follows.
∎

2.6.
One-point extensions

We recall the definition of a one-point extension of an algebra.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra and
M\in\modu\Lambda
. The
one-point extension
\Lambda[M]
of
\Lambda
by
M
is the algebra

\Lambda[M]=\begin{bmatrix}\K&M\\
0&\Lambda\end{bmatrix}.

Notice that if
\Lambda[M]
is the one-point extension of
\Lambda
by
M
, and if
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}
is a complete set of orthogonal primitive idempotents of
\Lambda
, then the Cartan matrix of
\Lambda[M]
is given by

(2.4)

C_{\Lambda[M]}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\dimv(M)^{T}\\
0&C_{\Lambda}\end{bmatrix}.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Let
M\in\modu\Lambda
be a module. Then the one-point extension
\Lambda[M]
of
\Lambda
by
M
has a presentation
\Lambda[M]=\K Q[\omega]/\I^{\prime}
with

\displaystyle Q[\omega]_{0}

\displaystyle=Q_{0}\cup\{\omega\},

\displaystyle Q[\omega]_{1}

\displaystyle=Q_{1}\cup\bigcup_{v\in Q_{0}}A_{v},

where
A_{v}
is a set of arrows
\omega\to v
which is in bijection with a basis of
(\topu M)e_{v}
.

Proof.

Follows by the definition of a quiver of a finite-dimensional algebra, see for example
[
ASS06
, Section II.3]
.
∎

3.
n
-levelled algebras

3.1.
Definition and basic properties

We recall the following notion.

Definition 3.1
.

[
Hil95
, Definition 3.1]

Let
Q
be a quiver.

(a)

We say that
Q
is
n
-levelled
if there is a surjective function
\level\colon Q_{0}\rightarrow\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
such that if
\alpha\colon v\rightarrow u
is an arrow in
Q
then
\level(v)=\level(u)+1
. For a vertex
v\in Q_{0}
we call
\level(v)
the
level
of
v
.

(b)

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a bound quiver algebra. We say that
\Lambda
is
n
-levelled
if
Q
is
n
-levelled. In this case, if
M\in\modu\Lambda
is a module, then we say that
M
is supported at level
i
if
M
is supported at a vertex
v
with
\level(v)=i
. We also denote

\displaystyle\maxlevel(M)

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\mid\text{$M$ is supported at level i}\},

\displaystyle\minlevel(M)

\displaystyle=\min\{i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\mid\text{$M$ is supported at level i}\}.

Note that the definition in
[
Hil95
]
has some additional assumptions which do not matter for us since all our quivers are connected.

Example 3.2
.

Let
G
be a bipartite loopless multigraph with a coloring
V=X\cup Y
. Let
Q
be the quiver with underlying graph
\overline{Q}=G
and bipartite orientation, that is the arrows in
Q
are oriented from the vertices in
X
to the vertices in
Y
. Define

\level(v)=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{if $v\in X$,}\\
0,&\mbox{if $v\in Y$.}\end{cases}

Then
Q
is a
1
-levelled quiver and
\K Q
is a
1
-levelled algebra.

We have the following easy observations.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be an
n
-levelled algebra. Then the following hold.

(a)

The quiver
Q
is acyclic and
\Lambda
is finite-dimensional.

(b)

Let
M\in\modu\Lambda
. Then

(b1)

\ell\ell(M)\leq\maxlevel(M)-\minlevel(M)+1
. Moreover, if there exists a path
p
from a vertex at level
\maxlevel(M)
to a vertex at level
\minlevel(M)
such that
Mp\neq 0
, then equality holds.

(b2)

\pdim(M)\leq\maxlevel(M)
. Moreover, if
\pdim(M)=n
, then
\maxlevel(M)=n
.

(b3)

\idim(M)\leq n-\minlevel(M)
. Moreover, if
\idim(M)=n
, then
\minlevel(M)=0
.

(c)

\gldim(\Lambda)\leq n
.

Proof.

That
Q
is acyclic follows immediately by the definition of an
n
-levelled quiver. Hence
\Lambda
is finite-dimensional and part (a) holds. Part (c) follows immediately from part (b). It remains to show part (b). Since
\Lambda
is
n
-levelled, it readily follows that

\minlevel(M)\leq\minlevel(\rad M)\leq\maxlevel(\rad M)\leq\maxlevel(M)-1.

Hence the inequality in (b1) follows by induction on
\maxlevel(M)-\minlevel(M)
and using
\ell\ell(M)=\ell\ell(\rad M)+1
. Furthermore, if such a path
p
as in (b1) exists, then

\rad^{\maxlevel(M)-\minlevel(M)}(M)=M\rad^{\maxlevel(M)-\minlevel(M)}(\Lambda)\supseteq Mp\neq 0,

and equality in (b1) holds.

Next, since
\Omega M\subseteq\rad M
, we also have that

\maxlevel(\Omega M)\leq\maxlevel(M)-1.

Hence
\pdim(M)\leq\maxlevel(M)
follows by induction on
\maxlevel(M)
and using
\pdim(M)=\pdim(\Omega M)+1
. Notice that since
\maxlevel(M)\leq n
, it follows that
\pdim M=n
implies
\maxlevel(M)=n
. This shows part (b2). Part (b3) follows similarly.
∎

The following statement establishes some connections between
n
-levelled algebras and the Koszul property.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be an
n
-levelled algebra. Then the following hold.

(a)

Let
v,u\in Q_{0}
. If
\Ext^{i}_{\Lambda}(e_{u}\Lambda_{0},e_{v}\Lambda_{0})\neq 0
implies that
i=\level(v)-\level(u)
, then
\Lambda
is Koszul.

Assume moreover that
\Lambda
is Koszul.

(b)

The Koszul dual
\Lambda^{!}
is
n
-levelled and we have a triangulated equivalence
\db(\modu\Lambda)\simeq\db(\modu\Lambda^{!})
given by a tilting complex
\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}}:=\bigoplus_{u\in Q_{0}}e_{u}\Lambda_{0}[n-\level(u)]
.

(c)

Let
\Lambda
be twisted
\tfrac{m}{l}
-Calabi–Yau with twisting automorphism
\phi
.
For a vertex
v\in Q_{0}
, let
\phi(v)\in Q_{0}
be the unique vertex such that
(e_{v}\Lambda)_{\phi}\simeq e_{\phi(v)}\Lambda
. If
\level(\phi(v))=\level(v)
for all
v\in Q_{0}
, then the Koszul dual
\Lambda^{!}
is twisted
\tfrac{m}{l}
-Calabi–Yau as well.

Proof.

(a)

This follows by
[
Hil95
, Lemma 3.1]
.

(b)

That
\Lambda^{!}
is
n
-levelled follows by the fact that its quiver is the opposite of the quiver of
\Lambda
.
For the second claim, we note that it can be found (implicitly) in
[
Hil95
]
, but we also include a proof for the convenience of the reader:
Observe that our assumption that
\Lambda
is Koszul combined with part (a) above implies that

\Hom_{\db(\modu\Lambda)}(\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}},\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}}[i])=0
for
i\neq 0
.
Since
\Lambda
is finite-dimensional we know that
\Thick(\Lambda_{0})=\db(\modu\Lambda)
. It is straightforward to check that
\Thick(\Lambda_{0})=\Thick(\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}})
, and this then proves the second claim as
\Lambda
is of finite global dimension.

(c)

Let
\epsilon_{i}=\sum_{\level(j)=i}e_{j}
. Since
\level(\phi(v))=\level(v)
for all
v\in Q_{0}
, we have that
\phi
acts as a bijection between the vertices of
Q_{0}
which are on the same level. Hence
(\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0})_{\phi}\simeq\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0}
. Then since
\Lambda
is twisted
\tfrac{m}{l}
-Calabi–Yau with twisting automorphism
\phi
we obtain

\nu^{l}_{\Lambda}(\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0})\simeq(\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0})_{\phi}[m]\simeq\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0}[m].

Now, recall that we let
\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}}=\bigoplus_{u\in Q_{0}}e_{u}\Lambda_{0}[n-\level(u)]
, and observe that
\epsilon_{i}\Lambda^{!}\simeq\mathbf{R}\Hom_{\Lambda}(\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}},\epsilon_{i}\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}})

where
\epsilon_{i}\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\level(j)=i}e_{j}\Lambda_{0}[n-i]
.
Consequently,
\epsilon_{i}\Lambda^{!}
is sent to (a cohomological shift of)
\epsilon_{i}\Lambda_{0}
by the quasi-inverse of
\mathbf{R}\Hom_{\Lambda}(\widetilde{\Lambda_{0}},-)
.
By the uniqueness of the Serre functor, we thus get that
\nu_{\Lambda^{!}}^{l}(\Lambda^{!})\simeq\Lambda^{!}[m]
, and so the claim follows by
[
HI11a
, Proposition 4.3(a)]
. ∎

Example 3.5
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a
2
-levelled algebra. Then
\I
is generated by quadratic relations and
\gldim\Lambda\leq 2
by Lemma
3.3
. Hence
\Lambda
is Koszul by Example
2.5
.

3.2.
n
-levelled algebras and higher homological algebra

In this section we investigate some properties of algebras which are both
n
-representation-finite and
n
-levelled. We start with some useful properties of indecomposable summands of the
n
-cluster tilting module of such an algebra.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be an
n
-representation-finite and
n
-levelled algebra for
n\geq 1
. Let
M=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\tau_{n}^{-i}(\Lambda)=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\tau_{n}^{i}(D\Lambda)
. Let
X
be an indecomposable summand of
M
, Then the following hold:

(a)

If
X
is not projective, then
\maxlevel(X)=n
.

(b)

If
X
is not injective, then
\minlevel(X)=0
.

(c)

If
X
is neither projective nor injective, then
\ell\ell(X)=n+1
.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is not projective. Then
\pdim(X)=n
by Remark
2.9
(a). Then part (a) follows by Lemma
3.3
(b2). Part (b) follows similarly. For part (c), we have by parts (a) and (b) that
X
is supported at level
n
and at level
0
. We write
X=(X_{v},f_{\alpha})
as a representation of
Q
bound by
\I
. By Lemma
3.3
(b1) it is enough to show that there exists a path
p
starting at a vertex at level
n
and terminating at a vertex at level
0
such that
f_{p}
is a nonzero linear map. For a vertex
v\in Q_{0}
let
U_{i}(v)
denote the set of all paths in
Q
starting at a vertex at level
i
and terminating at the vertex
v
. Consider the representation
Y=(Y_{v},g_{\alpha})
given by

Y_{v}=\sum_{p\in U_{n}(v)}\Img(f_{p})

and
g_{\alpha}
given by the restriction of
f_{\alpha}
to
Y_{s(\alpha)}
. It is easy to see that
Y
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
X
. Since
X
is indecomposable, it follows that
X\simeq Y
and so
Y
is supported at levels
n
and
0
. By definition of
Y
there exists a path
p
starting at a vertex at level
n
and terminating at a vertex at level
0
such that
g_{p}
is a nonzero linear map, as required.
∎

We continue with an important property of the indecomposable projective modules of the higher preprojective algebra in our setting.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\Lambda
be an algebra which is
n
-levelled,
n
-representation-finite and Koszul. Then the following hold.

(a)

All the indecomposable projective modules of the
(n+1)
-preprojective algebra
\Pi\coloneqq\Pi(\Lambda)
of
\Lambda
have the same Loewy length.

(b)

Let
v,u\in Q_{0}
be two vertices. If
\level(v)=\level(u)
, then
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)=\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{u}\Lambda)
.

Proof.

(a)

By
[
GI20
, Corollary 4.13]
we have that the algebra
\Pi
is selfinjective. By
[
GI20
, Theorem 4.21]
we have that
\Pi
is almost Koszul in the sense of
[
BBK02
]
. In particular,
\Pi
has only quadratic relations and thus necessarily has homogeneous relations with respect to the grading putting each arrow in degree
1
. Hence
\Pi
satisfies the requirements of
[
MV99
, Theorem 3.3]
from which the claim follows.

(b)

Let
w\in Q_{0}
be a vertex and
S=S(w)
be the simple
\Lambda
-module corresponding to
w
. By Lemma
3.3
(b2) we have that
\pdim(S)\leq n
and if
\pdim(S)=n
, then
\level(w)=n
. In this case it can be easily seen that if
e_{x}\Lambda
appears as a direct summand in the last nonzero term of the projective resolution of
S
, then
\level(x)=0
. It follows by
[
GI20
, Section 3]
that the quiver of
\Pi
is given by the quiver
Q
with possibly some more arrows from vertices at level
n
to vertices at level
0
(note that the referenced article uses left modules while we use right modules). Let
l_{v}^{\Pi}
be the length of a longest path
p_{v}^{\Pi}
in
\Pi
starting at the vertex
v
; similarly define
l_{u}^{\Pi}
and
p_{u}^{\Pi}
. Then
l_{v}^{\Pi}=\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)
and
l_{u}^{\Pi}=\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{u}\Pi)
and so by part (a) we obtain
l_{v}^{\Pi}=l_{u}^{\Pi}
. Since
\Lambda
is
n
-levelled, and by the description of the arrows in
\Pi
, the path
p_{v}^{\Pi}
is of the form
p_{v}^{\Pi}=\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}\cdots\alpha_{i+k}
where
\alpha_{j}
is an arrow from a vertex in level
j
to a vertex in level
j-1
or from a vertex in level
0
to a vertex in level
n
and similarly for
p_{u}^{\Pi}
. Consider the subpath
p_{v}^{\Lambda}
of
p_{v}^{\Pi}
given by considering only the arrows in the quiver
Q
and similarly define
p_{u}^{\Lambda}
. By their construction and since
\level(v)=\level(u)
, it follows that
p_{v}^{\Lambda}
and
p_{u}^{\Lambda}
have the same length.
Note that a path in
\Lambda
is nonzero if and only if it is nonzero as a path in
\Pi
since
\Lambda
can be considered as the degree
0
part of
\Pi
with respect to some grading.
Hence, it follows that
p_{v}^{\Lambda}
and
p_{u}^{\Lambda}
are paths of maximal length in
\Lambda
starting at
v
and
u
respectively. Since they have the same length, it follows that
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)=\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{u}\Lambda)
. ∎

We are especially interested in the following special cases of
2
-levelled quivers.

Definition 3.8
.

A
2
-levelled quiver
Q
is called
i
-light
if the level
i
is the only level which consists of exactly one vertex. A
2
-levelled bound quiver algebra
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
is called
i
-light
if
Q
is a
2
-levelled
i
-light quiver.

A crucial example for us is the following.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
r,s
be integers
\geq 1
. The
(r,s)
-star quiver

S_{(r,s)}
is the quiver

x_{1}
x_{r}
y_{1}
y_{s}
.
z
a_{1}
a_{r}
b_{1}
b_{s}

We say that a bound quiver algebra
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
is an
(r,s)
-star algebra
if
Q=S_{(r,s)}
.

In particular a quiver
Q
is
2
-levelled and
1
-light if and only if
Q=S_{(r,s)}
for some
r,s\geq 2
. Note also that for a
2
-levelled quiver
Q
we have that
Q
is
i
-light if and only if
Q^{\text{op}}
is
(2-i)
-light, and similarly for a
2
-levelled bound quiver algebra
\Lambda
. In the special case of
i
-light
n
-levelled algebras and under the assumption of
2
-representation-finiteness of
\Lambda
we know the Loewy length of indecomposable projective and indecomposable injective
\Lambda
-modules.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a
2
-levelled
i
-light and
2
-representation-finite finite-dimensional algebra. Let
v\in Q_{0}
be a vertex. Then the following hold.

(a)

\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)=\level(v)+1
.

(b)

\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda e_{v})=3-\level(v)

Proof.

(a)

If
\level(v)=0
, then
v
is a sink and the claim follows immediately. If
\level(v)=1
, then it is enough to show that there exists an arrow
\alpha\in Q_{1}
starting at
v
. Assume to a contradiction that
\level(v)=1
and there is no arrow from
v
to the unique vertex in level
0
. Then
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)=1
. It follows by Proposition
3.7
(b) that the indecomposable projective at every vertex at level
1
has Loewy length
1
. In particular, there exists no arrow from any vertex at level
1
to the vertex at level
0
, contradicting
Q
being connected and
2
-levelled.

Now assume that
\level(v)=2
. If
i=1
, then
Q=S_{(r,s)}
is a star quiver with
r,s\geq 2
. Let
\alpha:v\to z
be the unique arrow starting at the vertex
v
. By
[
HST24
, Lemma 4.24]
we have that there exist at least
2
arrows
\beta
,
\beta^{\prime}
starting at
z
such that
\alpha\beta
and
\alpha\beta^{\prime}
are nonzero in
\Lambda
. It follows that
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)
is at least
3
. Clearly it can be at most
3
which shows the claim in this case.

Now assume that
i\in\{0,2\}
. Since
\Lambda
is
0
-light if and only if
\Lambda^{\text{op}}
is
2
-light and using the duality
D
between
\modu\Lambda
and
\modu\Lambda^{\text{op}}
, it is enough to show the result for
i=0
. Hence assume that
i=0
. Clearly there exists an arrow starting at
v
since
Q
is
2
-levelled and connected. Hence
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)\geq 2
. By Lemma
3.3
(b1) it is enough to show that there exists a nonzero path from
v
to the unique vertex at level
0
. Assume to a contradiction that no such path exists. Then
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)=2
and by Proposition
3.7
(b) we conclude that the same is true for all vertices at level
0
. Hence
\Lambda
is a radical square zero algebra since
\Lambda
is
2
-levelled. Then the unique vertex
v
with
\level(v)=0
has at least
2
incoming arrows, since
\Lambda
is
2
-levelled
0
-light. But this contradicts
\Lambda
being
2
-representation-finite via
[
Vas23
, Lemma 2.3]
.

(b)

Notice that
\Lambda^{\text{op}}
is also an
n
-levelled algebra with
\level_{\Lambda^{\text{op}}}(v)=2-\level_{\Lambda}(v)
for a vertex
v\in Q_{0}
. Then using part (a) we have

\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda e_{v})=\ell\ell_{\Lambda^{\text{op}}}(e_{v}\Lambda^{\text{op}})=\level_{\Lambda^{\text{op}}}(v)+1=2-\level_{\Lambda}(v)+1=3-\level_{\Lambda}(v)

as required. ∎

The following is the main result for this section.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\Lambda=\K Q/\I
be a
2
-levelled
i
-light and
2
-representation-finite finite-dimensional algebra. Let
\sigma:Q_{0}\to Q_{0}
be as in Remark
2.9
(b). Let
v\in Q_{0}
be a vertex.

(a)

We have
\level(v)=\level(\sigma(v))
and
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)+\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda e_{\sigma(v)})=4
.

(b)

There exists some positive integer
\ell
such that
\Lambda
is
\ell
-homogeneous. In particular,
\Lambda
is twisted
\frac{2(\ell-1)}{\ell}
-Calabi–Yau.

(c)

The algebra
\Lambda
is Koszul and the Koszul dual
\Lambda^{!}
is
2
-levelled and
2
-representation-finite.

Proof.

Let
\ell_{v}>0
be as in Remark
2.9
(b). Let
\Pi=\Pi(\Lambda)
be the
3
-preprojective algebra of
\Lambda
. By
[
GI20
, Proposition 2.12]
we have that the Loewy length of
e_{v}\Pi
satisfies

(3.1)

\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)=\sum_{j=0}^{l_{v}-1}\ell\ell_{\Lambda}\left(\nu_{2}^{-j}(e_{v}\Lambda)\right).

By Remark
2.9
(d) we have that
\nu_{2}^{-j}(e_{v}\Lambda)=\tau_{2}^{-j}(e_{v}\Lambda)
for every
0\leq j\leq\ell_{v}-1
. If
0<j<\ell_{v}-1
, then
\tau_{2}^{-j}(e_{v}\Lambda)
is neither projective nor injective and so
\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(\tau_{2}^{-j}(e_{v}\Lambda))=3
by Proposition
3.6
(c). Hence (
3.1
) becomes

\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)=\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)+3(\ell_{v}-2)+\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda e_{\sigma(v)}).

Using Lemma
3.10
, we can rewrite the above as

(3.2)

\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)=4+\level(v)+3(\ell_{v}-2)-\level(\sigma(v)).

Moreover, by Proposition
3.7
(a) we have for any vertex
u\in Q_{0}
that

(3.3)

\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)=\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{u}\Pi).

(a)

For
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, let
Q_{0}^{j}=\{x\in Q_{0}\mid\level(x)=j\}
be the set of vertices in
Q
at level
j
.
Replacing (
3.2
) in (
3.3
) and viewing the equality modulo
3
we obtain

(3.4)

\level(v)-\level(\sigma(v))\equiv\level(u)-\level(\sigma(u))\mod{3}.

In particular, we have that
\level(v)=\level(u)
implies that
\level(\sigma(v))=\level(\sigma(u))
. Hence for any
j\in\{0,1,2\}
there exists
\sigma(j)\in\{0,1,2\}
such that
\sigma(Q_{0}^{j})\subseteq Q_{0}^{\sigma(j)}
. Since
Q_{0}
is the disjoint union of
Q_{0}^{0}
,
Q_{0}^{1}
and
Q_{0}^{2}
and
\sigma:Q_{0}\to Q_{0}
is a bijection, we conclude that
\lvert Q_{0}^{j}\rvert=\lvert Q_{0}^{\sigma(j)}\rvert
. Let
x\in Q_{0}
be the unique vertex with
\level(x)=i
. Then

\lvert Q_{0}^{\sigma(i)}\rvert=\lvert Q_{0}^{i}\rvert=\lvert\{x\}\rvert=1.

Since
Q
is an
2
-levelled
i
-light quiver, we obtain that
\sigma(i)=i
and so
\sigma(x)=x
. Since (
3.4
) holds for any two vertices in
Q_{0}
, for
u=x
we obtain that

\level(v)-\level(\sigma(v))\equiv 0\mod{3}.

Since
\level(v),\level(\sigma(v))\in\{0,1,2\}
, we conclude that
\level(v)=\level(\sigma(v))
for any
v\in Q_{0}
. Using this and Lemma
3.10
we obtain

\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(e_{v}\Lambda)+\ell\ell_{\Lambda}(D\Lambda e_{\sigma(v)})=\level(v)+1+3-\level(\sigma(v))=4,

which shows part (a).

(b)

By part (a) we have that (
3.2
) becomes

\ell\ell_{\Pi}(e_{v}\Pi)=4+3(\ell_{v}-2),

for every
v\in Q_{0}
. Replacing this in (
3.3
) we obtain that
\ell_{v}=\ell_{u}
for any
v,u\in Q_{0}
. Setting
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{v}
we obtain that
\Lambda
is
\ell
-homogeneous. Finally,
\Lambda
is twisted
\tfrac{2(\ell-1)}{\ell}
-Calabi–Yau by Theorem
2.12
.

(c)

We have that
\Lambda
is Koszul by Example
3.5
. Hence the Koszul dual
\Lambda^{!}
is Koszul by Proposition
3.4
(b).

Next let
v\in Q_{0}
be a vertex. By part (b) we have that
\Lambda
is
\ell
-homogeneous. Hence Remark
2.9
(b) and (d) give that

(3.5)

\nu_{2}^{\ell-1}(D\Lambda e_{v})\simeq\tau_{2}^{\ell-1}(D\Lambda e_{v})\simeq e_{\sigma^{-1}(v)}\Lambda.

On the other hand, by part (b) we have that
\Lambda
is twisted
\tfrac{2(\ell-1)}{\ell}
-Calabi–Yau. Using this and
\nu_{2}=\nu\circ[-2]
we compute

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{2}^{\ell-1}(D\Lambda e_{v})&=(\nu\circ[-2])^{\ell-1}(D\Lambda e_{v})\simeq\nu^{-}\nu^{\ell}(D\Lambda e_{v})[-2(\ell-1)]\\
&\simeq\nu^{-}(D\Lambda e_{\phi(v)})[-2(\ell-1)][2(\ell-1)]\simeq e_{\phi(v)}\Lambda.\end{split}

Comparing (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) we see that
e_{\sigma^{-1}(v)}\Lambda\simeq e_{\phi(v)}\Lambda
from which we conclude that the bijections
\sigma:Q_{0}\to Q_{0}
and
\phi:Q_{0}\to Q_{0}
are inverse to each other. By part (a) we conclude that the conditions of part (c) of Proposition
3.4
are satisfied and so
\Lambda^{!}
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau of dimension
\tfrac{2(\ell-1)}{\ell}
.
Since
\gldim(\Lambda^{!})=2
, the claim follows by Theorem
2.12
(b). ∎

4.
One-point extensions of bipartite hereditary algebras

Our aim in this section is to derive a formula for the Coxeter polynomial of a one-point extension of a
1
-levelled algebra which satisfies certain conditions. Note that the Coxeter polynomial of a one-point extension algebra has been computed in
[
Hap09
]
.

4.1.
Setup

For the rest of the section we work under the following setup.

Setup 1
.

We consider a quadruple
(Q,M,\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
where
Q
is a quiver with at least one arrow,
M\in\modu\K Q
is a nonzero module and
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\RR
such that:

(i)

The underlying graph
\overline{Q}
of
Q
is a bipartite loopless multigraph with coloring
X\cup Y
and
Q
has bipartite orientation as in Example
3.2
. In particular
Q
is a
1
-levelled quiver. Moreover, we denote the vertices in
X
by
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\}
and the vertices in
Y
by
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{s}\}
and we order them by

x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{r}<y_{1}<\cdots<y_{s-1}<y_{s}.

(ii)

\topu(M)
is supported only in level
1
. We set
\Gamma=\K Q[M]
and
d_{M}=\dimv(M)^{T}
. Furthermore, we set

d_{x}=(\dim_{\K}(Me_{x_{1}}),\dots,\dim_{\K}(Me_{x_{r}}))^{T}\text{ and }d_{y}=(\dim_{\K}(Me_{y_{1}}),\dots,\dim_{\K}(Me_{y_{s}}))^{T},

where
e_{x_{1}},\dots,e_{x_{r}},e_{y_{1}},\dots,e_{y_{s}}
are the idempotents of
\K Q
corresponding to the vertices
x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{s}
.

(iii)

(d_{x},d_{y})
is a bi-eigenvector of
A(\overline{Q})
with bi-eigenvalue
(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
.

By part (i) in Setup
1
we have that the adjacency matrix
A(\overline{Q})
has the form

A(\overline{Q})=\begin{bmatrix}0_{r}&R\\
R^{T}&0_{s}\end{bmatrix}

where
R
is an
r\times s
-matrix with nonnegative integer entries.
By part (ii) in Setup
1
we have
d_{M}=\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}
. We collect some properties that we have in this setting.

Proposition 4.1
.

(a)

The Cartan matrix of
\K Q
respectively
\Gamma
is

C_{\K Q}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r}&R\\
0_{s}&I_{s}\end{bmatrix}\text{ respectively }C_{\Gamma}=\begin{bmatrix}1&d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\\
0_{r}&I_{r}&R\\
0_{s}&0_{s}&I_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

The Coxeter polynomial of
\K Q
respectively
\Gamma
is

\CP_{\K Q}(t)=\det(tC_{\K Q}+C_{\K Q}^{T})\text{ respectively }\CP_{\Gamma}(t)=\det(tC_{\Gamma}+C_{\Gamma}^{T}).

(b)

The algebra
\Gamma
is a
2
-levelled
0
-light Koszul algebra.

(c)

\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\QQ
,
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\in\ZZ
and
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}>0
.

(d)

d_{x}\neq 0
and
d_{y}\neq 0
. In particular,
d_{x}
is an eigenvector of
RR^{T}
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
and
d_{y}
is an eigenvector of
R^{T}R
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
.

(e)

\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}
.

(f)

The largest eigenvalue of
A(\overline{Q})
is
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
.

Proof.

That the Cartan matrix of
\K Q
has the form

(4.1)

C_{\K Q}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r}&R\\
0_{s}&I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

follows immediately by part (i) of Setup
1
. Using (
2.4
) and (
4.1
), we compute the Cartan matrix of
\Gamma
to be

(4.2)

C_{\Gamma}=\begin{bmatrix}1&d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\\
0_{r}&I_{r}&R\\
0_{s}&0_{s}&I_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that in particular we have
\det(C_{\K Q})=1
and
\det(C_{\Gamma})=1
. Then we have

\displaystyle\CP_{\Gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=\det(tI-(-C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{\Gamma}^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\det(tC_{\Gamma}+C_{\Gamma}^{T})\det(C_{\Gamma}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\det(tC_{\Gamma}+C_{\Gamma}^{T}),

and similarly for
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
. This shows part (a). That
\Gamma
is
2
-levelled
0
-light follows from Lemma
2.15
and using the assumption that
\topu(M)
is supported only in level
1
. Then
\Gamma
is Koszul by Example
3.5
. This shows part (b). Parts (c), (d), (e) and (f) follow by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

4.2.
The Coxeter polynomial of
\Gamma

In this section we continue working under Setup
1
and we compute the Coxeter polynomial
\CP_{\Gamma}(t)
of
\Gamma
. We need the following standard result about determinants of block matrices for our computations.

Proposition 4.2
.

[
Ber09
, Propositions 2.8.3 and 2.8.4]

Consider the block matrix

\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}
.

(a)

If
A
is invertible, then

\det\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}=\det(A)\det(D-CA^{-1}B)
.

(b)

If
D
is invertible, then

\det\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}=\det(D)\det(A-CD^{-1}B)
.

In the sequel, we set

(4.3)

w(t)\coloneqq(t+1)^{2}-t\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}=t^{2}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}-2)t+1

We have the following trivial observation.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let

f_{\pm}(t)=\frac{(t^{2}-2)\pm t\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}{2}.

Then the roots of
w(t)
are
f_{+}(\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}})
and
f_{-}(\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}})
. In particular,
-1
is not a root of
w(t)
.

Proof.

That these are the roots follows by a direct computation. Also, evaluating
w(t)
at
-1
we obtain
w(-1)=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
which is nonzero by Proposition
4.1
(c).
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The polynomial
w(t)
is a factor of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.1
(a) we have

\displaystyle\CP_{\K Q}(t)=\det(tC_{\K Q}+C_{\K Q}^{T})=\det\begin{bmatrix}(t+1)I_{r}&tR\\
R^{T}&(t+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

For
t\neq-1
and using Proposition
4.2
we obtain

\displaystyle\CP_{\K Q}(t)

\displaystyle=\det((t+1)I_{r})\det((t+1)I_{s}-R^{T}((t+1)I_{r})^{-1}tR)

\displaystyle=(t+1)^{r}\det((t+1)I_{s}-(t+1)^{-1}tR^{T}R)

\displaystyle=(t+1)^{r}\det((t+1)^{-1}((t+1)^{2}I_{s}-tR^{T}R))

\displaystyle=(t+1)^{r-s}\det((t+1)^{2}I_{s}-tR^{T}R)

Now notice that
R^{T}Rd_{y}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}d_{y}
implies

((t+1)^{2}I_{s}-tR^{T}R)d_{y}=w(t)d_{y}.

By Proposition
4.1
(c) we have that
d_{y}\neq 0
. Hence if
\lambda
is a root of
w(t)
, we obtain that the matrix
(\lambda+1)^{2}I_{s}-\lambda R^{T}R
has
w(\lambda)=0
as an eigenvalue, and so

\det((\lambda+1)^{2}I_{s}-\lambda R^{T}R)=0.

It follows that all roots of
w(t)
are roots of
\det((t+1)^{2}I_{s}-tR^{T}R)
. Since by Lemma
4.3
we have that
-1
is not a root of
w(t)
, and since
w(t)
is a monic polynomial we conclude that
w(t)
is a factor of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

If
z
is not a root of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
, then we have

\displaystyle w(z)\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}(z+1-z\Sigma_{1})+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}(z+1-\Sigma_{2}).

Proof.

If
z
is not a root of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
, then

0\neq\CP_{\K Q}(z)=\det\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

and so the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

is invertible. First, using
Rd_{y}=\Sigma_{1}d_{x}
and
R^{T}d_{x}=\Sigma_{2}d_{y}
, we compute

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1-z\Sigma_{1})d_{x}\\
(z+1-\Sigma_{2})d_{y}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=w(z)\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}.

By multiplying both sides of (
4.4
) by

\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

we obtain

\displaystyle w(z)\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1-z\Sigma_{1})d_{x}\\
(z+1-\Sigma_{2})d_{y}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}(z+1-z\Sigma_{1})+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}(z+1-\Sigma_{2}),

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

We have

\CP_{\Gamma}(t)=\frac{\CP_{\K Q}(t)}{w(t)}p(t),

where

p(t)=(t+1)(t^{2}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}-2)t+1).

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.1
(a), we have

\displaystyle\CP_{\Gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=\det(tC_{\Lambda}+C_{\Lambda}^{T})=\det\begin{bmatrix}(t+1)&td_{x}^{T}&td_{y}^{T}\\
d_{x}&(t+1)I_{r}&tR\\
d_{y}&R^{T}&(t+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

Assume that
z
is not a root of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
. Then the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

is invertible and so we may apply Proposition
4.2
(b) to continue our computation

\displaystyle\CP_{\Gamma}(z)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)&zd_{x}^{T}&zd_{y}^{T}\\
d_{x}&(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
d_{y}&R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}\left((z+1)-z\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\CP_{\K Q}(z)\left((z+1)-z\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}\right).

Assume that
z
is not a root of
w(t)
either. Using Lemma
4.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle w(z)\CP_{\Gamma}(z)

\displaystyle=\CP_{\K Q}(z)\left((z+1)w(z)-zw(z)\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\CP_{\K Q}(z)\left((z+1)w(z)-z\left(\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}(z+1-z\Sigma_{1})+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}(z+1-\Sigma_{2})\right)\right).

Set

r(t)=(t+1)w(t)-t\left(\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}(t+1-t\Sigma_{1})+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}(t+1-\Sigma_{2})\right).

We claim that
r(t)=p(t)
. Indeed, by expanding
r(t)
we obtain

r(t)=t^{3}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}-3)t^{2}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-3)t+1.

Using
\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}
which holds by Proposition
4.1
(e), we obtain

r(t)=(t+1)(t^{2}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}-2)t+1)=p(t),

as claimed. Hence we have shown that for all but finitely many
z\in\CC
(namely the roots of
w(t)
and
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
) we have

w(z)\CP_{\Gamma}(z)=\CP_{\K Q}(z)p(z).

Since
w(t)
,
\CP_{\Gamma}(t)
and
p(t)
are all polynomials, we obtain an equality of polynomials

w(t)\CP_{\Gamma}(t)=\CP_{\K Q}(t)p(t).

Since by Lemma
4.4
we have that
w(t)
is a factor of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
, the claim follows by dividing both sides by
w(t)
.
∎

We end the section with the following result, giving a correspondence between the eigenvalues of the Coxeter matrix of
\K Q
and the eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix of the associated bipartite graph
A=A(\overline{Q})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\CP_{A}(t)
be the characteristic polynomial of
A=A(\overline{Q})
and let
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
be the Coxeter polynomial of
\K Q
. Then the roots of
\CP_{A}(t)
come in pairs of the form
\{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}\}
, the roots of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
come in pairs of the form
\{z,z^{-1}\}
, and furthermore there is a correspondence between these pairs of roots. The correspondence is given by

\{\lambda,-\lambda\}\mapsto\{z=f_{+}(\lambda),z^{-1}=f_{-}(\lambda)\},

and

\{z,z^{-1}\}\mapsto\{\lambda=\sqrt{z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}},-\lambda\},

where
f_{\pm}(t)
is as in Lemma
4.3
.

This can be found stated in the beginning of Section 3 of
[
MS05
]
.
For the reader’s convenience, we have included a proof.

Proof.

First of all it is straightforward to compute that the maps defined in the statement are inverse to each other. Also, since
\overline{Q}
is a bipartite graph, the roots of
\CP_{A}(t)
are symmetric around
0
.

Now, using Proposition
4.2
(a) we compute

\displaystyle\CP_{A}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{bmatrix}\lambda I_{r}&-R\\
-R^{T}&\lambda I_{s}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det(\lambda I_{r})\det(\lambda I_{s}-(-R^{T})(\lambda I_{r})^{-1}(-R))

\displaystyle=\lambda^{r}\det(\lambda I_{s}-\lambda^{-1}R^{T}R).

Making the substitution
\lambda=\sqrt{z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}
, we obtain

(4.5)

\CP_{A}\left(\sqrt{z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\right)=(z+1)^{r}z^{-\frac{r}{2}}\det((z+1)z^{-\frac{1}{2}}I_{s}-(z+1)^{-1}z^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{T}R).

On the other hand, using Proposition
4.1
(a) we have

\displaystyle\CP_{\K Q}(z)=\det(zC_{\K Q}+C_{\K Q}^{T})=\det\begin{bmatrix}(z+1)I_{r}&zR\\
R^{T}&(z+1)I_{s}\end{bmatrix}.

Using Proposition
4.2
(a) we obtain

\displaystyle\CP_{\K Q}(z)

\displaystyle=\det((z+1)I_{r})\det((z+1)I_{s}-R^{T}((z+1)I_{r})^{-1}zR)

\displaystyle=(z+1)^{r}\det((z+1)I_{s}-(z+1)^{-1}zR^{T}R)

\displaystyle=(z+1)^{r}\det(z^{\frac{1}{2}}((z+1)z^{-\frac{1}{2}}I_{s}-(z+1)^{-1}z^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{T}R))

\displaystyle=(z+1)^{r}z^{\frac{s}{2}}\det((z+1)z^{-\frac{1}{2}}I_{s}-(z+1)^{-1}z^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{T}R)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:q(sqrt(t)+1/sqrt(t)})}{=}(z+1)^{r}z^{\frac{s}{2}}\frac{\CP_{A}\left(\sqrt{z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\right)}{(z+1)^{r}z^{-\frac{r}{2}}}

\displaystyle=z^{\frac{r+s}{2}}\CP_{A}\left(\sqrt{z}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\right).

Hence, we see that roots of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
indeed come in pairs
\{z,z^{-1}\}
and that every pair of roots
\{z,z^{-1}\}
of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
corresponds to a pair of roots
\{\lambda,-\lambda\}
of
\CP_{A}(t)
as claimed.
∎

4.3.
One-point extensions and higher homological algebra

In this section we continue working under Setup
1
. Our aim is to use the Coxeter polynomial of
\Gamma
to obtain necessary conditions that have to be satisfied if
\Gamma
has certain properties coming from higher homological algebra.
First we obtain a condition on the graph
\overline{Q}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau. Then
\overline{Q}
is a reflexive graph. Furthermore,
\overline{Q}
is a Salem graph if and only if
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}>2
.

Proof.

Let

(4.6)

\CP_{A}(t)=\det\begin{bmatrix}tI_{r}&-R\\
-R^{T}&tI_{s}\end{bmatrix}

be the characteristic polynomial of
A=A(\overline{Q})
. Recall that by Proposition
4.1
(f) we have that
\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
is the largest eigenvalue of
A
. Hence it is enough to show that all other eigenvalues belong to
[-2,2]
. Since eigenvalues of
A
are symmetric around
0
, it is enough to show that if
\lambda>0
is a root of
\CP_{A}(t)
with
\lambda\neq\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
, then
\lambda\leq 2
.

Assume towards a contradiction that there exists a root
\lambda
of
\CP_{A}(t)
with
\lambda>2
and
\lambda\neq\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
, and recall the correspondence between the roots of
\CP_{A}(t)
and
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
described in Proposition
4.7
.
In particular,
z=f_{+}(\lambda)
where
f_{+}(t)
is as in Lemma
4.3
, is a root of
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
. Since
\lambda\neq\sqrt{\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}}
, Lemma
4.3
implies that
z
is not a root of
w(t)
. We conclude by Lemma
4.6
that
z
is a root of
\CP_{\Gamma}(t)
. Since
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau, we conclude that
\lvert z\rvert=1
by Proposition
2.13
. But then

1=\lvert z\rvert=\lvert f_{+}(\lambda)\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{\left(\lambda^{2}-2\right)+\lambda\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}\right\rvert>\frac{(4-2)+2\sqrt{4-4}}{2}=1,

is a contradiction.
∎

Next we obtain a condition on the data
(\lvert d_{x}\rvert,\lvert d_{y}\rvert,\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
. We start with the following easy lemma.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
s(t)=t^{2}+at+b
be a polynomial such that
a,b\in\QQ
and all roots of
s(t)
are on the unit circle. Then
s(t)\in\{t^{2}+1,t^{2}\pm t+1,t^{2}\pm 2t+1\}
.

Proof.

Let
z
and
\overline{z}
be the two roots of
s(t)
in
\CC
. Since by assumption we have
\lvert z\rvert=1
it follows that
z=\cos\theta+i\sin\theta
where
0\leq\theta<2\pi
. Then

t^{2}+at+b=s(t)=(t-z)(t-\overline{z})=t^{2}-(z+\overline{z})t+z\overline{z}=t^{2}-2\cos\theta t+1.

This shows that
2\cos\theta\in\QQ
and that
b=1
. Hence
\cos\theta\in\QQ
and so, by Niven’s theorem
[
Niv56
]
, we obtain that
\cos\theta\in\{0,\pm\tfrac{1}{2},\pm 1\}
. The claim follows.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau. Then the following hold.

(a)

\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\QQ
,
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\in\ZZ
and
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}>0
.

(b)

d_{x}\neq 0
and
d_{y}\neq 0
. In particular,
d_{x}
is an eigenvector of
RR^{T}
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
and
d_{y}
is an eigenvector of
R^{T}R
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}
.

(c)

\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}
.

(d)

\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
.

Proof.

Parts (a), (b) and (c) are just parts (c), (d) and (e) of Proposition
4.1
. It remains to show part (d). Notice that by Lemma
4.6
we have

(4.7)

w(t)\CP_{\Gamma}(t)=\CP_{\K Q}(t)(t+1)q(t),

where

q(t)\coloneqq t^{2}-(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}-2)t+1.

Since by Lemma
4.4
we have that
w(t)
divides
\CP_{\K Q}(t)
, we conclude that the roots of
q(t)
are roots of
\CP_{\Gamma}(t)
. Since
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau, it follows that the roots of
\CP_{\Gamma}(t)
lie on the unit circle from Proposition
2.13
. Hence the roots of
q(t)
lie on the unit circle as well. By part (a) we have that
q(t)
is a rational polynomial. Then by Lemma
4.9
we obtain that

q(t)\in\{t^{2}+1,t^{2}\pm t+1,t^{2}\pm 2t+1\}.

and the claim follows.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is
2
-representation-finite. Then the statements (a)–(d) of Proposition
4.10
hold.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.1
(b) we have that
\Gamma
is a
2
-levelled
0
-light algebra. Since
\Gamma
is also
2
-representation-finite, by Proposition
3.11
(b) we have that
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau, which is the requirement of Proposition
4.10
.
∎

4.4.
The order of the negative of the Coxeter matrix

We finish this section with a small computation that will be useful later. As before we work under Setup
1
. Then the Cartan matrix of
\Gamma
is of the form

C_{\Gamma}=\begin{bmatrix}1&d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\\
0_{r,1}&I_{r}&R\\
0_{s,1}&0_{s,r}&I_{s}\end{bmatrix},

where
R
is the adjacency matrix of the underlying graph of the quiver
Q
and where
(d_{x}^{T},d_{y}^{T})
is the dimension vector of
M
. In the sequel, whenever we have an identity matrix
I_{k}
, we simply write
I
; the dimension
k
is always clear from context. Our aim is to compute the order of the matrix
-\Phi_{\Gamma}
under certain assumptions.

Let us first set
C_{+}:=C_{\Gamma}-I
. Since
R
is bipartite, we have that
C_{+}^{3}=(C_{\Gamma}-I)^{3}=0
, and we thus have that

C_{\Gamma}^{-1}=C_{+}^{2}-C_{+}+I=\begin{bmatrix}0&0_{1,r}&d_{x}R^{T}\\
0_{r,1}&0_{r,r}&0_{r,s}\\
0_{s,1}&0_{s,r}&0_{s,s}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0&d_{x}^{T}&d_{y}^{T}\\
0_{r,1}&0_{r,r}&R\\
0_{s,1}&0_{s,r}&0_{s,s}\end{bmatrix}+I.

Recall that the Coxeter matrix of
\Gamma
is given by
\Phi_{\Gamma}=-C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{\Gamma}^{-1}
.
Hence, using that
Rd_{y}=\Sigma_{1}d_{x}
and
R^{T}d_{y}=\Sigma_{2}d_{y}
, we can compute

\displaystyle C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{\Gamma}^{-1}

\displaystyle=C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{+}^{2}-C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{+}+C_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&-d_{x}^{T}&(\Sigma_{2}-1)d_{y}^{T}\\
d_{x}&-d_{x}d_{x}^{T}+I&(\Sigma_{2}-1)d_{x}d_{y}^{T}-R\\
d_{y}&-d_{y}d_{x}^{T}+R^{T}&(\Sigma_{2}-1)d_{y}d_{y}^{T}-R^{T}R+I\end{bmatrix}.

From this, we get

C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{\Gamma}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}a\\
bd_{x}\\
cd_{y}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a-b\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+c(\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}\\
d_{x}(a+b(1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2})+c((\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{1})\\
d_{y}(a+b(\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2})+c((\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+1))\end{bmatrix}.

Let now

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\\
f_{3}\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}(\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{1}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+1\end{bmatrix},

so that

(4.8)

(C_{\Gamma}^{T}C_{\Gamma}^{-1})^{m}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
d_{x}\\
d_{y}\end{bmatrix}

holds if and only if

(4.9)

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix}^{m}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}

holds. With this in mind we have the following.

Proposition 4.12
.

Assume that
\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}
and that
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}=p
for some
p\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
. Then the following hold:

(a)

If
p\neq 4
, then the negative of the Coxeter matrix
\Phi_{\Gamma}
is of order described in the following table:

p

Order of
-\Phi_{\Gamma}

0

\infty

1

6

2

4

3

3

(b)

If
p=4
, then
(r,\Sigma_{1},s,\Sigma_{2})=(4,2,4,2)
.

Proof.

Using the equivalence of (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), it is enough to show that

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix}^{m}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}

for
m>0
never happens if
p=0
, happens first for
m=6
for
p=1
, for
m=4
for
p=2
, for
m=3
for
p=3
, and only ever happens if
(\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2},\Sigma_{1},\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2},\Sigma_{2})=(4,2,4,2)
for
p=4
, in which case it happens first for
m=4
. This can be showed using
\Sigma_{1}\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}=\Sigma_{2}\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}
and that
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}+\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}+\lvert d_{y}\rvert^{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}\Sigma_{1}=p
and by noting that

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}p\\
p\\
p\end{bmatrix},

that

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}p\\
p\\
p\end{bmatrix},

and that

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Indeed, if
p=0
and
M
is the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{1}\\
1&1-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{2}\\
1&\Sigma_{2}-\lvert d_{x}\rvert^{2}&f_{3}\\
\end{bmatrix},

an easy induction gives that

M^{m}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=m\cdot\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}m(m+1)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\\
(\Sigma_{2}-1)\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

The rest of the cases are similar.
∎

The additional conditions in Proposition
4.12
are of course not arbitrary: they are always satisfied when
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau by Proposition
4.10
. Hence we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.13
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is twisted fractionally Calabi–Yau. Then the assumptions and results of Proposition
4.12
hold.

5.
2-representation-finite quadratic monomial algebras

Our aim in this section is to classify certain quadratic monomial
2
-hereditary algebras. This is achieved by relating star algebras to one-point extensions of the form described in Setup
1
.

5.1.
Trivial extensions of star algebras

We start by introducing some notation about star algebras. Let
S_{(r,s)}
be an
(r,s)
-star quiver as in (
1.1
) and let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be an
(r,s)
-star algebra. In particular
\Lambda
is
2
-levelled, and if
r,s\geq 2
then
\Lambda
is also
1
-light. For
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
we define the set
\mathcal{Z}(x_{i})=\{b_{j}\mid a_{i}b_{j}\in\I\}
and for
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
we define the set
\mathcal{Z}(y_{j})=\{a_{i}\mid a_{i}b_{j}\in\I\}
. Note that the relations generating
\I
are necessarily monomial and can be described by a subset of
\{1,\ldots,i\}\times\{1,\ldots,j\}
. We encode this information in a quiver
B_{\Lambda}
as follows:

•

The set of vertices of
B_{\Lambda}
is the set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
.

•

The arrows of
B_{\Lambda}
are of the form
\beta_{ji}:y_{j}\to x_{i}
whenever
a_{i}b_{j}\not\in\I
.

Notice that the underlying graph of
B_{\Lambda}
is bipartite with partition
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}\cup\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
.

Definition 5.1
.

We say that a star algebra
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
is
balanced
if every arrow in
S_{(r,s)}
appears in at least one nonzero path of length
2
in
\Lambda
and in at least one zero path of length
2
in
\Lambda
.

Note that a star algebra
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
is balanced if and only if
1\leq\mathcal{Z}(x_{i})\leq s-1
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and
1\leq\mathcal{Z}(y_{j})\leq r-1
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
.

Recall that the
trivial extension
of a finite-dimensional algebra
A
is the symmetric algebra
T(A)
given by
A\oplus D(A)
as an
A
-bimodule and endowed with the multiplication induced by letting
D(A)
square to zero, i.e. by setting
(a,f)\cdot(a^{\prime},g):=(aa^{\prime},ag+fa^{\prime})
for
(a,f),(a^{\prime},g)\in T(\Lambda)
. In the case of balanced star algebras, we can compute the quiver with relations of the trivial extension of an
(r,s)
-star algebra using the following proposition.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be a balanced
(r,s)
-star algebra where
\I=(\rho)
. Then
T(\Lambda)=Q_{T(\Lambda)}/(\rho_{T(\Lambda)})
where

(i)

Q_{T(\Lambda)}
can be obtained by adjoining to the quiver
S_{(r,s)}
the arrows of the quiver
B_{\Lambda}
, i.e. by adjoining an arrow
\beta_{ji}\colon y_{j}\rightarrow x_{i}
for every non-zero path
a_{i}b_{j}
in
\Lambda
, and

(ii)

\rho_{T(\Lambda)}
can be obtained by adjoining to
\rho
the sets of relations given by

\{\beta_{ji}a_{i}-\beta_{ji^{\prime}}a_{i^{\prime}}\,\lvert\,a_{i}b_{j},a_{i^{\prime}}b_{j}\not\in\rho\,\,\text{for}\,\,j\,\,\text{such that}\,\,1\leq j\leq s\},

and

\{b_{j}\beta_{ji}-b_{j^{\prime}}\beta_{j^{\prime}i}\,\lvert\,a_{i}b_{j},a_{i}b_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\rho\,\,\text{for}\,\,i\,\,\text{such that}\,\,1\leq i\leq r\},

and the set of all paths of length greater than
3
.

Proof.

The computation of the quiver and relations of the trivial extension of an algebra can be found in
[
FP02
]
; see also the related works
[
Sch99
]
and
[
FST+22
]
.
∎

In other words, if
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
is a balanced
(r,s)
-star algebra, then the quiver
Q_{T(\Lambda)}
of
T(\Lambda)
has the form

(5.1)

x_{1}
x_{r}
y_{1}
y_{s}
x_{1}
x_{r}
,
z
B_{\Lambda}
a_{1}
a_{r}
b_{1}
b_{s}

where we identify the two copies of the vertices
x_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and the boxed area is a copy of the quiver
B_{\Lambda}
.

In certain cases we may obtain two algebras with isomorphic trivial extensions as per the following result.

Theorem 5.3
.

[
FP06
, Theorem A]

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional algebra such that all cycles in the quiver of
A
are zero in
A
, let
\J
be an ideal of
T(A)
generated by exactly one arrow from each non-zero cycle in
T(A)
, and let
A^{\prime}=T(A)/\J
. Then
T(A)\simeq T(A^{\prime})
.

We can apply Theorem
5.3
in the case of a balanced star algebra as in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be a balanced
(r,s)
-star algebra. Let
\J
be the ideal of
T(\Lambda)
generated by
\{a_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq r\}
and define the algebra

(5.2)

\Gamma_{\Lambda}=T(\Lambda)/\J.

Then the following hold.

(a)

\Gamma_{\Lambda}=\K B_{\Lambda}[M]
where
M
is the unique indecomposable
B_{\Lambda}
-module with dimension vector
(1,\ldots,1)^{T}
.

(b)

T(\Lambda)\simeq T(\Gamma_{\Lambda})
.

Proof.

(a)

By definition, the quiver
Q_{\Gamma_{\Lambda}}
is the quiver of
T(\Lambda)
with the arrows
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
removed. In other words, by (
5.1
) we conclude that
Q_{\Gamma_{\Lambda}}
has the form

y_{1}
y_{s}
x_{1}
x_{r}
.
z
B_{\Lambda}
b_{1}
b_{s}

Moreover, by Proposition
5.2
we see that the only relations in
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
are commutativity relations. More specifically, any two distinct paths in
Q_{T(\Lambda)}
starting at the vertex
z
and terminating at a vertex
x_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
are of the form
\beta_{ji}b_{j},\beta_{j^{\prime}i}b_{j^{\prime}}
, and we have
\beta_{ji}b_{j}-\beta_{j^{\prime}i}b_{j^{\prime}}\in\rho_{T(\Lambda)}
. It follows that
\beta_{ji}b_{j}-\beta_{j^{\prime}i}b_{j^{\prime}}\in\rho_{\Gamma_{\Lambda}}
as well. Then
\Gamma_{\Lambda}=\K B_{\Lambda}[M]
follows by a direct computation.

(b)

In view of Theorem
5.3
we need to show that the set
\{a_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq r\}
consists of exactly one arrow from each non-zero cycle in
T(\Lambda)
. Since all paths of length greater than
3
in
T(\Lambda)
are zero, the only nonzero cycles in
\Lambda
are of the form
a_{i}b_{j}\beta_{ji}
with
a_{i}b_{j}\not\in\I
. In particular, every nonzero cycle in
\Lambda
has exactly one arrow
a_{i}
. Since
\Lambda
is balanced, each
a_{i}
belongs to at least one cycle, and the claim follows. ∎

We will need a way of relating the spectrum of the Coxeter matrices of
\Lambda
and
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
.
It turns out that the algebras
\Lambda
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\Lambda}
are derived equivalent, and this is sufficient as derived equivalent algebras have the same Coxeter polynomials; see e.g.
[
Hap09
, Lemma 4.1]
.

To show that
\Lambda
and
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
are derived equivalent, we use a tilting complex that is Koszul dual to a
2
-APR tilting complex in the sense of
[
IO11
, Definition 3.14]
.
Nevertheless, it takes less work to simply show that it is a tilting complex from scratch than to use Koszul duality and the results in
[
IO11
]
.
Recall that we let
\epsilon_{i}=\Sigma_{\level(j)=i}e_{j}
for
e_{j}
a primitive idempotent for
\Gamma
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\Lambda=\K S_{(r,s)}/\I
be a balanced
(r,s)
-star algebra. Let
X:=\nu(\epsilon_{0}\Gamma_{0})\oplus\epsilon_{2}\Gamma_{0}\oplus\epsilon_{1}\Gamma_{0}[1]
be a complex in
\D^{b}(\modu\Gamma)
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The Koszul dual
\Lambda^{!}
is the bound quiver algebra with quiver

y_{1}
y_{s}
x_{1}
x_{r}
.
z
b_{1}^{\ast}
b_{s}^{\ast}
a_{1}^{\ast}
a_{r}^{\ast}

and relations given by all paths
b_{j}^{\ast}a_{i}^{\ast}
such that
a_{i}b_{j}\not\in I
. In particular,
B_{\Lambda^{!}}
is the bipartite complement of
B_{\Lambda}
.

(b)

X
is a tilting complex;

(c)

\End_{\D^{b}(\modu\Gamma)}(X)\simeq\Lambda^{!}
; and

(d)

\Gamma=\Gamma_{\Lambda}
is derived equivalent to
\Lambda
.

Proof.

(a)

As we have mentioned, in Section
2.4
, the quiver of
\Lambda^{!}
is given by the opposite quiver of
S_{(r,s)}
. This shows the claim about the quiver of
\Lambda!
.
The description of the relations follows by the description of the quadratic dual of
\Lambda
as a quotient of a path algebra of a quiver with relations; see e.g.
[
BGS96
, Definition 2.8.1]
.
In particular, since there are only (quadratic) monomial relations,
\Lambda^{!}
is also quadratic monomial, and its relations are exactly those quadratic monomials that are not relations for
\Lambda
.

(b)

Since
\epsilon_{0}\Gamma_{0}
is projective, we know
\nu(\epsilon_{0}\Gamma_{0})
is injective and thus to check that it is rigid, it suffices to check that

\Hom_{\D^{b}(\modu\Gamma^{)}}(\nu(\epsilon_{0}\Gamma_{0}),(\epsilon_{2}\Gamma_{0}\oplus\epsilon_{1}\Gamma_{0}[1])[i\neq 0])=0

and that
